On your **Slack question** first: For DataQ's alerting (which today is the `ResultPublisher` → Teams adaptive-card path with a webhook secret in Key Vault), adding Slack is a near-mirror. What's needed depends on which mechanism: - **Slack Incoming Webhook (recommended, matches the Teams pattern):** just **one webhook URL** — `https://hooks.slack.com/services/T…/B…/…`. You create a Slack app, enable *Incoming Webhooks*, and add it to a channel; the channel is bound into the URL. We'd store it as a Key Vault secret (e.g. `SLACK_WEBHOOK_SECRET_NAME`, parallel to `TEAMS_WEBHOOK_SECRET_NAME`) and POST Block Kit JSON. **No bot token, no channel ID required.** - **Slack Web API (`chat.postMessage`)** — only if you need threading, message edits, or @-mentions by user ID: a **bot token** (`xoxb-…`), the **`chat:write`** scope, and the **channel ID**. So for the simplest "fire an alert into a channel," all you need is the **incoming webhook URL**. Tell me which mechanism and whether it's workspace-wide or per-suite (like the #373/#378 per-suite notification config) and I can scope it as a new `ResultPublisher` channel. > DEVELOPER

Now that we are going to cloud, is plain python+pip better than conda?

> AGENT

Good question to raise now — the 2.84 GB image, the painful amd64 emulated build, and the cross-internet GHCR pull latency (ADR 0023) are all symptoms that point at conda. Let me ground my answer in how DataQ actually manages deps before I opine:

> AGENT

This is well worth doing, and the evidence is decisive. Look at what `environment.yml` actually installs: ```yaml dependencies: - python=3.13 - pip - pip: - -r backend/requirements-dev.txt # ← everything ``` **Conda installs nothing from conda channels except the Python interpreter and pip itself.** All ~30 runtime deps come through pip wheels already. So conda's one real advantage — prebuilt binary packages from conda-forge — is **completely unused** here. You're paying conda's cost for none of its benefit. ## Verdict: yes, `python:3.13-slim` + pip is better for the cloud target **Concrete wins — and they're exactly the pain we just hit:** - **Image size:** `miniconda3` base (~600 MB) + the env → your **2.84 GB** image. `python:3.13-slim` + these wheels (multi-stage) → roughly **0.8–1.2 GB**. Smaller image = faster ACA cold-start/scale-out and less cross-internet GHCR pull latency — the precise concern ADR 0023 flagged. - **Build:** simpler and much faster under the amd64 emulation we just fought; a multi-stage build drops the compiler/toolchain from the final layer. - **No compilation risk:** every heavy dep (pyarrow, pandas, `snowflake-connector-python`, `databricks-sql-connector`, `great-expectations`, `psycopg2-binary`) ships manylinux wheels. (requirements.txt even already notes "swap psycopg2-binary for psycopg2 in the production image.") ## Two scopes — I'd recommend the first now 1. **Slim the production image only (low risk, big win):** convert `backend/Dockerfile` to multi-stage `python:3.13-slim` + `pip install -r requirements.txt`; keep conda for local dev. Image shrinks immediately, dev workflow untouched. Minor cost: a dev(conda)/prod(pip) split. 2. **Full switch to venv+pip everywhere:** retire `environment.yml`, update `setup.sh`/CI/docs, drop the "conda only" rule. Cleaner long-term, bigger blast radius. ## The catch "Conda only" is a **locked Week-1 decision** (CLAUDE.md §6/§11). Flipping it properly needs an **ADR superseding that lock** (exactly like ADR 0017 superseded the Python-3.11 lock) plus updates to CONTRIBUTING/CLAUDE.md — so I won't change it silently. …

Good question — and for DataQ specifically, App Service has a real downside. Here's the honest comparison: ## App Service (Web App for Containers) **Benefits** - **Full separation** — its own App Service Plan, nothing shared with the harness (satisfies "but that's it" cleanly). - Mature platform niceties: deployment slots (staging→prod swap), easy custom domain/TLS, simple autoscale. - Managed identity → Key Vault works identically; SWA *can* link an App Service backend, so the same-origin `/api` proxy still works. **Downsides (these bite DataQ)** - **The Celery worker doesn't fit.** App Service Linux containers must answer on an HTTP port or the platform's health check marks them unhealthy and restart-loops them. A Celery worker has no HTTP listener → you're forced into hacks (dummy HTTP server, or disabling health checks) or a separate awkward always-on web app. ACA treats a non-HTTP worker as a first-class app; App Service fights it. - **Redis broker → Azure Cache for Redis.** App Service can't run our self-hosted `redis:7-alpine` sidecar cleanly, so you'd add managed Redis (~$16/mo Basic C0) — exactly the cost we avoided. - **No native job primitive** for `alembic upgrade head` — migrations become a startup hook or a manual one-off, less clean than the ACA migrate Job. - **More cost + rework**: App Service Plan + managed Redis, and a meaningful rewrite of the Terraform (diverges from ADR 0024). ## Shared harness ACA env (the recommended option) **Benefit:** keeps the entire architecture intact — worker, self-hosted redis, and the migrate Job are all first-class; cheapest; immediate. **Downside:** the compute *environment* is shared with the harness (shared env-level Log Analytics + internal network). The apps/KV/Postgres/identity stay separate and tagged. Notably, the shared internal network is exactly why I already password-protected redis — so that risk is mitigated. App lands in West US …

Name or service not known` → webserver 500 + tasks stuck `queued` then failing. No code/Terraform change — state already matched reality. ## Verified - Webserver `/health` → **200**; `/` and `/home` → **302** (normal redirect-to-login). No more `OperationalError` / host-translation errors. - Worker reconnected: `Connected to redis://dataq-harness-redis:6379/0` … `celery@… ready.` — tasks will be consumed again from the next scheduled cycle. This was all in the **harness** (your out-of-repo demo env), so nothing in the DataQ repo changed. One thing worth flagging for next time: **any delete-recreate of the shared Postgres needs a restart of every dependent Container App** (DataQ's `dataq-app-api`/`-worker` too, if their secrets are refreshed the same way) — the secret update alone isn't enough. > AGENT

Let me read the event DAG in full — its `*/10` cron looks schedule-y but the comments say it's the "event half."

> AGENT

They're **not** all schedule-based — there's a deliberate 3-schedule + 1-event mix. The catch is that the event one *also* carries a cron, so in the UI it looks identical. Here's the actual split: | DAG | `schedule=` | Real trigger modality | |---|---|---| | `flow_a_snowflake_load` | `30 1 * * *` | **Schedule** — daily clock load | | `flow_a_uc_reference` | `45 1 * * *` | **Schedule** — daily | | `flow_b_medallion` | `0 2 * * *` | **Schedule** — daily | | `flow_a_payments_event` | `*/10 * * * *` | **Event / arrival-driven** (file sensor) | ## Why the event DAG still has a cron `flow_a_payments_event` is the event half. Its `*/10` cron is **not a load cadence — it's the poke/wake cadence**. Each wake, a `PythonSensor` (reschedule mode, 120s pokes, 540s timeout) checks ADLS `snowflake/payments/` for newly-landed files: - **file landed** → `load_payments` runs (the "event" fired); - **nothing in the 10-min window** → `soft_fail=True` → the sensor **skips**, no load. So the *trigger* is file arrival; the cron just bounds how often it looks. That's Airflow's documented **file-sensor** pattern — event semantics implemented on a polling wake-up. Airflow has no truly "scheduleless" entry point for a sensor DAG, which is exactly why it visually reads as schedule-based. ## Two honest caveats 1. **It's poll-based, not true push.** Genuinely event-based would be **Event-Grid (blob-created) → Airflow REST `POST /dagRuns`** (or a Dataset/Asset trigger). The harness `airflow.tf` even names "Event-Grid (file-landing push)" as the intended ingress, but the DAG as written uses a sensor poll, not a push trigger. The 10-min poll is the demo-grade stand-in. 2. **This is the harness, not DataQ.** These DAGs just generate pipeline runs for DataQ to *observe*. DataQ's own event path is genuinely event-driven — the Airflow `on_success/on_failure_callback → POST …

Switching context - Why the date / timestamp fields are stored in varchar / string datatype [both sf and uc]?

> AGENT

Good DQ question — let me confirm the actual cause in the load path and generator rather than guess. Checking how feeds are read/typed and how the generator emits temporal columns.

> AGENT

Confirmed the cause from the code. Let me verify the UC reference path's read format too, so the "both SF and UC" answer is precise:

> AGENT

That refines the picture importantly: the UC reference feeds are **Parquet** (which *preserves* dtypes), yet you still see strings — so for UC the cause must be upstream in the generator. Let me confirm how the generator types its temporal columns:

> AGENT

Confirmed the full mechanism end-to-end. Short answer: **it's an artifact of the load path + a pandas dtype gotcha, not an intentional modeling choice.** Here's the precise chain. ## The generator types them fine — as Python objects In [generate.py](../DataQ-harness/mockdata/generate.py), temporal fields are built as Python `datetime` objects, in two flavors: - `*_ts` (e.g. `order_ts`, `paid_ts`, `load_ts`) → `datetime.datetime` → pandas infers **`datetime64[ns]`**. - `*_date` (e.g. `signup_date`, `order_date`, `start_date`, `snapshot_date`) → built with `.date()` → `datetime.date` → pandas keeps these as **`object`** dtype. This is the classic pandas trap: a column of `datetime.date` is *not* promoted to `datetime64`, it stays object (= effectively string-like). ## Then the load path erases/loses the type **Snowflake (all 12 feeds are CSV — [config.py:79-91](../DataQ-harness/mockdata/config.py#L79), [flow_a_snowflake_load.py:20](../DataQ-harness/airflow/dags/flow_a_snowflake_load.py#L20)):** 1. `read_feed(..., "csv")` does a plain `pd.read_csv(buf)` with **no `parse_dates`** ([_harness.py:49](../DataQ-harness/airflow/dags/_harness.py#L49)). CSV carries no types, and `read_csv` infers numbers but leaves *every* date/timestamp as `object`. 2. `load_to_snowflake` → `write_pandas(auto_create_table=True)` ([_harness.py:79](../DataQ-harness/airflow/dags/_harness.py#L79)) infers the DDL from those dtypes → `object` → **VARCHAR**. → In Snowflake, **every** temporal column (both `_ts` and `_date`) is VARCHAR, because CSV round-trips through text. **Unity Catalog (reference dims are Parquet — [config.py:93-101](../DataQ-harness/mockdata/config.py#L93)):** Parquet *preserves* dtypes, but `_PANDAS_TO_SQL` only promotes `datetime64[ns]`→TIMESTAMP; everything else falls through to the `STRING` default ([_harness.py:103-118](../DataQ-harness/airflow/dags/_harness.py#L103)). - `_ts` columns (datetime64) → **TIMESTAMP** ✅ - `.date()` columns (object) → **STRING** ❌ → So in UC it should be **only the DATE columns** (e.g. `date_dim.date`, `promotions.start_date/end_date`, `exchange_rates.rate_date`) that land as STRING — the `_ts` columns ought to be real TIMESTAMP. If you're seeing `_ts` columns as string in UC too, that's worth a closer look. ## Is it a bug? For a **DQ demo harness, loosely-typed dates are arguably useful** — string dates are a real-world smell and give you something to write checks against (castability, format/regex validation, "freshness on a VARCHAR needs `TO_TIMESTAMP` first"). …

The chain: 1. Generator emits `created_ts` as a real `datetime.datetime` ([generate.py:654](../DataQ-harness/mockdata/generate.py#L654)). 2. `emit.py` writes feedback as NDJSON with `df.to_json(..., date_format="iso")` ([emit.py:42](../DataQ-harness/mockdata/emit.py#L42)) → `created_ts` is serialized to an **ISO string** in the JSON. 3. The notebook does `spark.read.json(...).saveAsTable(raw.feedback)` — schema-on-read ([medallion_notebook.py:33](../DataQ-harness/databricks/medallion_notebook.py#L33)). **Spark's JSON reader does not infer timestamps by default** (`spark.sql.json.inferTimestamp=false` since Spark 3.0), so an ISO-looking string stays **STRING**. 4. Silver just dedups → type unchanged. ## This refines what I told you earlier I'd said UC `_ts` columns "ought to be TIMESTAMP." That holds **only for the parquet reference path** (where datetime64 survives). You pointed at exactly the case that breaks it: the **JSON bronze path stringifies the timestamp** regardless. So there are really *three* roads to string-typed temporals here: | Path | Feeds | Mechanism | |---|---|---| | CSV → Snowflake | 12 SF feeds | CSV erases all types → VARCHAR | | Parquet → UC | reference dims | `datetime.date` → object → STRING (datetime64 `_ts` *do* get TIMESTAMP) | | **JSON bronze → UC** | **feedback, sales_events** | ISO string in NDJSON + Spark `inferTimestamp` off → STRING | ## Fix for the JSON path, if you want it typed - Quickest: `spark.read.option("inferTimestamp", "true").json(...)` (re-infers ISO → TIMESTAMP), or - Explicit bronze schema (`StructType` with `TimestampType`) on `read.json` — authoritative, no inference cost, and the …

> DEVELOPER

How many different access types are there for dataq?

> AGENT

I'll look at how access is modeled in the codebase rather than guess.

> AGENT

DataQ has access on **two orthogonal axes**, which together give it **4 per-suite levels + 1 workspace-wide flag**. ## 1. Per-suite access — 4 levels (the main "access types") Defined in [suite_authz.py](backend/app/services/suite_authz.py#L33-L38), gated by [models.py:41](backend/app/db/models.py#L41) `PERMISSIONS = ("view", "edit", "admin")`: | Level | How it's held | Capabilities | |---|---|---| | **view** | `shares` row | read the suite, its checks, its results | | **edit** | `shares` row | + create/update/delete checks, update suite, **trigger runs** | | **admin** | `shares` row | + manage shares (grant/revoke) **and** delete the suite | | **owner** | implicit (`suite.created_by`) | same as admin, but immutable — can't be revoked/demoted, and can't be granted a share | Only **3 are grantable** (`view`/`edit`/`admin`, the `Share.permission` enum); **owner** is the 4th, implicit and non-grantable. Ranked `view(1) < edit(2) < admin(3) < owner(4)`. ## 2. Workspace-admin — 1 orthogonal flag Separate from suite sharing: a config allowlist (`WORKSPACE_ADMIN_EMAILS`) surfaced as `is_workspace_admin` on `/me`. It bypasses owned-or-shared scoping for the `/admin/{suites,users,access}` endpoints (the Admin control centre, #289). It's **not** a per-suite level — it's a global capability axis with no Azure/Entra claim read. --- So the precise answer depends on what you mean by "access type": - **Grantable suite permissions:** 3 (`view`, `edit`, `admin`) - **Effective suite levels:** 4 (the above + `owner`) - **Plus** the orthogonal **workspace-admin** flag (global, not a suite level) The Admin page's **"Access"** tab lists the actual `shares` grant rows across the workspace.

> DEVELOPER

Can you spin up some (~ 5-6) users [Entra ID] with different level of access [or is it even possible?]

> AGENT

Good question — let me check what's actually possible here, because there are two separate layers (Entra = *authentication*, DataQ shares = *authorization*) and they have a chicken-and-egg dependency. Let me investigate both before proposing anything.

> AGENT

Let me check feasibility against the live tenant (read-only) — whether the signed-in Azure principal can create directory users, and what domain they'd live in.

> AGENT

Default domain exists (`royarijit04outlook.onmicrosoft.com`, verified). Now checking whether the signed-in principal can actually create directory users.

> AGENT

Confirmed feasibility. Here's the situation: **Yes, it's possible.** I'm **Global Administrator** in your tenant (`07b592a3…`), and there's a verified domain (`royarijit04outlook.onmicrosoft.com`), so I can create directory users via `az ad user create`. Directory has 2 users today; adding ~6 is free (Entra free tier). **But there's an important architectural split** — Entra and DataQ access are two different layers: | Layer | What it controls | How it's set | |---|---|---| | **Entra ID** | *Authentication* — who can log in | `az ad user create` (I can do this) | | **DataQ suite shares** (view/edit/admin) | *Authorization* — what a logged-in user can touch | `shares` rows in the `dataq` DB — **per suite**, granted by an owner/admin | | **Workspace-admin** | Global "see everything" flag | `WORKSPACE_ADMIN_EMAILS` env var on the API (prod config change + restart) | The catch: `grant_share` ([share_service.py:87](backend/app/services/share_service.py#L87)) requires the target to **already exist as a DataQ user** — and DataQ users are JIT-created only on **first login** ([auth.py:70](backend/app/core/auth.py#L70), keyed on the Entra `oid`). So to wire up access without making each person log in first, I'd **pre-seed** the `users` + `shares` rows directly in the `dataq` Postgres DB using each new user's real `oid` (idempotent — their first login just updates the row). > DEVELOPER

How does the the option B for MFA works? Also does MFA works only with secutity questions?

> AGENT

Good questions — let me clear up both, including a common misconception in the second one. ## How Option B (disabling Security Defaults) works **Security Defaults** is a single tenant-wide on/off policy that Microsoft turns **on** for new tenants. While it's on, it forces three things: 1. **All users must register for MFA** (within 14 days of first sign-in) and get challenged when Microsoft deems the sign-in risky. 2. **Admins are always MFA-challenged** every sign-in. 3. **Legacy auth protocols are blocked.** **Disabling it** is one toggle in the portal: > **Entra admin center** (entra.microsoft.com) → **Identity → Overview → Properties** → bottom link **"Manage security defaults"** → set the dropdown to **Disabled** → pick a reason → **Save**. Once it's off, and because your tenant has **no Entra ID P1** (so no Conditional Access policies can re-impose MFA), there is **nothing left enforcing a second factor**. New users then sign in with **just username + password** — the only first-login step is the **forced password change** (because I create them with `forceChangePasswordNextSignIn`). No Authenticator, no phone, no extra prompt. Effects to be aware of: - It's **tenant-wide** — it also drops MFA for **you** (`royarijit04`) and any future user. Fine for a throwaway demo tenant; a real security downgrade otherwise. - **Reversible** anytime by flipping the same toggle back to Enabled. - I **can't do it from the CLI** (token lacks `Policy.ReadWrite.SecurityDefaults`, and it's a tenant-wide security change I shouldn't make unilaterally) — it's a ~30-second portal action on your side. ## Does MFA work only with security questions? **No — and importantly, security questions are *not* an MFA method at all.** That's a common mix-up: - **Security questions** are only used for **SSPR** (Self-Service Password *Reset*) — recovering a forgotten password. They are **never** accepted as a second factor …

> DEVELOPER

What will happen if I keep MFA off while creating the users and later toggel it on?

> AGENT

**Security Defaults is evaluated live at each sign-in — it's not stamped onto users at creation time.** So toggling it on later applies retroactively to everyone, including the users you made while it was off. Concretely: **While it's off (create + use):** - Users are created normally; no MFA registration is forced. - They sign in with **password only** (plus the one-time forced password change on first login, which is independent of MFA). **When you flip it back on later:** - The policy immediately covers **all** users, including those 6 — they are **not grandfathered**. - Each will be pushed into the **"More information required"** flow to register Microsoft Authenticator/phone, with the standard **14-day grace period** to do it. - After the grace window (or immediately for any admin-role user, and on any sign-in Microsoft flags as risky), an un-enrolled user is **blocked until they enroll**. - Anyone who *had* already registered an MFA method stays fine — no re-enrollment. **Net:** turning it on later doesn't break the accounts, but it does mean each demo user will get nagged to set up Authenticator on subsequent logins (and can be hard-blocked once the 14 days lapse). Nothing you've seeded in DataQ is affected — Security Defaults only governs Entra *authentication*, never the DataQ `shares`/access rows. **Practical takeaways for a demo:** - **Most stable:** leave it **off** for the life of the demo → password-only the whole time. - **If you must turn it back on:** do it knowing each user hits a one-time Authenticator enrollment within 14 days. Turning it back **off** again clears the enforcement. - The forced **password change** on first login happens either way — if you'd rather skip even that, tell me and I'll create the users with `forceChangePasswordNextSignIn = false`. > AGENT

You're exactly right, and you've found a genuine product gap. Here's what's happening and why: ## What the redactor does today `redact_sample_failures` ([run_service.py:373](backend/app/services/run_service.py#L373)) is **value-based blanket masking**: it keeps only two summary keys — `unexpected_count` and `unexpected_percent` — and `<redacted>`s **every other value** in the failing rows. It preserves the *shape* (row count + column names) but masks all cell values — so **LINE_TOTAL (the offending value) and ORDER_NUMBER (the identifier) are both masked**, even though neither is PII. ## Why it was built blunt (the real rationale) From the code's own docstring: it can't know *which* columns are PII ahead of time — a `name`/`email`/`ssn` could be any column in any table — so it masks everything as the safe default, deliberately *not* key-based ("masking only known-PII keys would leak any un-enumerated column"). It's **secure but blunt.** ## Why your critique is correct A failing-row sample where **both the bad value and the identifier are redacted is not actionable** — you can't see *what* was wrong or *which* row. That defeats the entire purpose of a sample. For a data-quality tool, "here are the rows that failed" *is* the value, and right now it delivers "some rows failed, can't tell you anything about them." So: **only PII should be redacted, and a real identifier must survive** — exactly your point. The blunt default traded away the feature's usefulness to be safe. ## The right fix — surgical, column-aware redaction The blanket approach is wrong; the fix is **column classification** (and there's already a documented seam for it at that call site — "a future opt-in could surface raw rows"): 1. **Classify columns** as *PII* (redact) vs *safe* (show) — per connection/suite, with a sensible auto-guess (column-name heuristics like the log redactor's `email/ssn/phone/name`) that you can override. 2. **Designate an …

That GHCR screenshot is the package→repo connect (already done — CI can push). Now to your questions — all three are good calls, and the first two have a payoff you might not expect. ## Detection: metadata/tags first, then column name — yes, and it's *better* than you think Snowflake and Databricks both ship **native data classification** that maps almost perfectly onto what we need: - **Snowflake** `SYSTEM$CLASSIFY` / classification returns a **`privacy_category`** per column ∈ `{IDENTIFIER, QUASI_IDENTIFIER, SENSITIVE}` — that's *literally* "identifier column" vs "PII column", authoritative from the warehouse. Plus object **tags**. - **Unity Catalog** has **column tags** + `system.information_schema.column_tags` and classification. So the layering you described is exactly right, and there's a clean precedence: 1. **Datasource tags/classification** (authoritative — Snowflake `privacy_category`, UC tags) 2. **Column-name heuristics** (fallback — reuse the log redactor's `email/ssn/phone/name/...`) 3. **Manual override** (what the user sets) 4. **Default-redact** anything still unclassified (so security never regresses) ## Suite-level metadata — agree, with one nuance Storing **identifier column + PII columns** at the suite level is the right pragmatic v1 (a suite targets one table, so it's natural config). The only nuance: PII-ness is really a property of the *table/column*, not the suite — so two suites on the same table would re-declare it. I'd build it suite-level now, but shape the field so it can **promote to a connection/column catalog later** without a rewrite. Net: **suite-level, yes.** ## Notifications — yes, they're skeletal; enrich them Today's alert has suite/datasource/target/severity/failing-check-names. I'd add: a **deep link to the run** (click straight to run-detail), **per-check expected-vs-observed + metric value**, the **actionable sample row** (once redaction is fixed — `ORDER-1041: line_total = −12.50`), **env / duration / triggered-by**, and tighter severity styling. Yes — but it's **Airflow's *own* RBAC, not Azure RBAC.** Important distinction: ## Airflow has RBAC, but it's application-level Airflow (2.x) ships RBAC via **Flask App Builder** — users + roles live in Airflow's **own metadata database**, completely separate from Azure AD / Entra. While betterleaks finishes (it's been the slow job both runs), here's the answer to your GX question — it's ready and relevant to last-week planning. ## How much of GX Core are we actually using? **Short version: a thin, deliberately-curated slice — 7 of GX's ~50+ built-in expectations — but the runner is generic, so expanding is nearly free.** The key architectural fact: **there is no server-side expectation allowlist.** [gx_runner.py](backend/app/datasources/gx_runner.py#L39-L47) takes whatever `expectation_type` string it's given, title-cases it to a GX class, and instantiates it. The *only* gate on which expectations exist is the frontend's [expectationCatalog.ts](frontend/src/components/checks/expectationCatalog.ts) — a 7-entry curated list. So adding an expectation is usually a **catalog entry, not a backend change**. **What we use today:** | Using | GX feature | |---|---| | ✅ 7 expectations | not-null, unique, between, in-set, value-length, match-regex, table-row-count | | ✅ Custom SQL | `UnexpectedRowsExpectation` (ADR 0019) | | ✅ now (#426) | freshness/volume — **non-GX**, raw SQL aggregates | | ❌ deliberately skipped | Data Docs (we have our own Results page, ADR 0018), Checkpoints/Actions (we use Celery + ResultPublisher), Data Assistant profiler (we built our own) — all correct calls | **High-ROI built-ins we're *not* using (all cheap — catalog-only):** 1. **`mostly` parameter** — the biggest gap. Tolerance on *any* existing expectation ("95% not-null"). Right now every check is all-or-nothing. GX still computes `unexpected_percent`, so it works with our ADR-0016 severity banding **with zero backend change**. This is the single highest-ROI add. 2. **Compound/cross-column** — `expect_compound_columns_to_be_unique` (multi-col primary keys!), `expect_column_pair_values_a_to_be_greater_than_b` (e.g. `end_date > start_date`), `expect_multicolumn_sum_to_equal`. Real reconciliation-lite, no backend change. 3. **Type & format** — `expect_column_values_to_be_of_type` / `in_type_list`, `expect_column_values_to_match_strftime_format` (date validity). 4. **Set relations** — `expect_column_distinct_values_to_be_in_set` / `to_contain_set` (referential-integrity-lite on a known domain). 5. **Aggregate stats** — `expect_column_{mean,median,sum,stdev,min,max}_to_be_between`. These produce a **numeric metric naturally** → great for the trend …

Verdict up front, then per-item. **The big enabler:** there's no server-side expectation allowlist — [gx_runner.py](backend/app/datasources/gx_runner.py#L39-L47) instantiates *any* `expectation_type` string, and config is free-form JSONB. So "wire in a check" is mostly a **frontend-catalog + validation** problem, not a backend-engine problem. That's why these are cheaper than they look. --- ### 1. Implement all 5 high-ROI built-ins **Feasibility: High · Effort: S–M · No new infra.** - 4 of 5 (`mostly`, compound/cross-column, type, set-relations) are **catalog-only** entries in [expectationCatalog.ts](frontend/src/components/checks/expectationCatalog.ts). `mostly` is one optional field. - **The catch is the 5th — aggregates** (mean/sum/stdev between). They don't emit `unexpected_percent`; their result is a scalar in `observed_value`, and they're **two-sided** (too low *or* too high fails). That's the *volume-monitor* pattern, not the GX unexpected-% pattern. So aggregates are better modeled through the **monitor-kind `metric_value` path #426 just built** than through GX banding. Worth deciding deliberately — they may be a 3rd monitor kind, not a GX expectation. ### 2. LLM SQL generator for `UnexpectedRowsExpectation` **Feasibility: High · Effort: M · Best-aligned of the four.** - The guardrails already exist: ADR 0019 gives custom-SQL **read-only single-statement validation + SQL-datasource gating + least-privilege role**, and **dry-run preview** lets the user verify before save. - Key principle: **trust LLM-generated SQL no more than user SQL** — run it through the *same* validator + dry-run. That neutralizes the obvious "LLM writes `DROP TABLE`" risk for free. - Real risk is *correctness* (hallucinated columns), mitigated by feeding **schema + column-profiler stats** as context. We already have the profiler. ### 3. LLM curated check suggestions for a suite **Feasibility: High · Effort: M.** - This is "GX Data Assistant, but LLM-driven and constrained to *our* vocabulary." Done — documented and PR'd as **#430** (docs-only, milestoned Backlog). ## Direct answer to your LLM-integration question My recommendation: **an admin-configured, default-off, bring-your-own-credential `LLMProvider` seam** — not per-user keys. Reasoning, mapped to your options: | Your option | Verdict | |---|---| | **User brings own key** | ❌ as primary — friction for a single-tenant team tool; the features run async in the worker. Keep as an optional power-user *override* later. | | **Admin sets it up** | ✅ **the model.** Mirrors how connections/secrets already work: workspace-admin configures **one** provider, credential in the `SecretStore` (Key Vault = one impl). | | **Local LLM (HF pull)** | ✅ but reframed: we **don't** bake a model server / HF download into the image (GPU/size/ops, against slim-image ADR 0025). Customer runs **Ollama/vLLM/TGI** (where they pull the HF model) and points DataQ at its **OpenAI-compatible endpoint**. "Local" = an endpoint impl. | | **Azure Foundry / Bedrock** | ✅ first-class impls behind the same seam, for customers already on Azure/AWS. | So the seam has **4 concrete impls**: Anthropic (default, per CLAUDE.md) · Azure AI Foundry · AWS Bedrock · any OpenAI-compatible URL (covers all local). Most speak OpenAI-compatible, so the seam is basically `{base_url, api_key, model, auth_adapter}` + a capability flag for structured output. Three non-negotiables I baked in: 1. **No key-proxy.** BYOL (ADR 0013) → customer pays via their own credential; we never route through an Anthropic key *we* own (that's hosted-SaaS + our cost). 2. **Data residency / PII.** Send the LLM **schema + aggregate profiler stats only — never raw rows / PII columns** (reuse the redaction seam). The local-endpoint impl exists exactly for can't-leave-network customers. 3. **Default-off + graceful degradation** — the app fully works with no LLM configured. It runs in the **Celery worker** (already holds secret access), …

Important framing first, then the audit. **Scope & disclaimer:** I can audit the **technical controls** in the code against what these regulations technically require. I **can't certify legal compliance** — that's a determination for counsel/DPO, and a large share of GDPR/HIPAA is *organizational* (BAAs, DPAs, DPIAs, consent, lawful basis, breach process), not code. One thing that helps enormously: **DataQ is BYOL/customer-deployed (ADR 0013)** — the customer is the *data controller*, deploys into their own tenant/region, owns the data and (post-v1) the LLM key. That pushes many obligations to them and makes DataQ a *processor*. Also: **HIPAA only applies if a customer points DataQ at PHI**, and **GDPR only to EU personal data** — DataQ is a generic DQ tool, so PII/PHI exposure is *incidental* (failing-row samples), not a core people-database. ## What's genuinely strong (privacy-by-design — GDPR Art 25) | Control | Where | Maps to | |---|---|---| | **Logger-level PII redaction**, key-based — credentials + contact PII + **AAD object IDs explicitly tagged GDPR Art 4(1)** (2026-05-28 audit) | [logging.py:14-57](backend/app/core/logging.py#L14-L57) | GDPR Art 32, 25 | | **Default-redact sample failures**, column-aware (policy + heuristic) | [run_service.py:440-563](backend/app/services/run_service.py#L440-L563) | GDPR Art 25, 5(1)(c) minimization | | **Retention purge** of `sample_failures` (30d default, keeps only the non-PII metric), with an auditable `purged_at` | [tasks.py:361](backend/app/worker/tasks.py#L361), config | GDPR Art 5(1)(e) storage limitation | | **Secrets seam**, never in tracked files / never logged; TLS `sslmode=require` | [secrets.py](backend/app/core/secrets.py), [postgres.tf:30](deploy/terraform/postgres.tf#L30) | GDPR Art 32 / HIPAA §164.312 | | **Suite-scoped access control** + workspace-admin allowlist + Azure AD SSO | authz + #289 | GDPR Art 32 / HIPAA access control | | **Config-change history** (Type-4 snapshot tables) | ADR 0020 | GDPR Art 5(2) accountability (partial) | ## Real gaps (honest, with severity) 1. **🔴 No data-*access* …

The doc is explicit that those stay the controller's. The BYOL controller/processor split (ADR 0013) is what makes the claim defensible — and it dovetails with the LLM seam design from earlier (schema-only, PII-redacted, local-endpoint, region-pinned), which is itself a forward compliance control (#434).
